Riemannian Optimization and the Hartree–Fock Method
Caio Oliveira da Silva

Abstract




I dedicate this thesis to my grandmother (
in memoriam
), to my father and to Yuri.

I was actually chasing a special sort of buzz, a special moment that comes sometimes. One teacher called these moments “mathematical experiences.” What I didn’t know then was that a mathematical experience was aesthetic in nature, an epiphany in Joyce’s original sense. These moments appeared in proof-completions, or maybe algorithms. Or like a gorgeously simple solution to a problem you suddenly see after filling half a notebook with gnarly attempted solutions. It was really an experience of what I think Yeats called “the click of a well-made box.” Something like that. The word I always think of it as is “click.”

— David Foster Wallace

Abstract

In the present work we studied a subfield of Applied Mathematics called Riemannian Optimization.
The main goal of this subfield is to generalize algorithms, theorems and tools from Mathematical Optimization to the case in which the optimization problem is defined on a Riemannian manifold.

As a case study, we implemented some of the main algorithms described in the literature (Gradient Descent, Newton–Raphson and Conjugate Gradient) to solve an optimization problem known as Hartree–Fock.
This method is extremely important in the field of Computational Quantum Chemistry and it is a good case study because it is a problem somewhat hard to solve and, as a consequence of this, it requires many tools from Riemannian Optimization.
Besides, it is also a good example to see how these algorithms perform in practice.

Keywords
: Riemannian Optimization, Hartree–Fock Method, Mathematical Optimization, Quantum Chemistry, Riemannian Geometry.

1
Introduction

Riemannian Optimization is a field that aims to study optimization problems given by

f:M\to\mathbf{R},

(0.1)

being
M
a Riemannian manifold.
(In Chapter
2
we will define precisely what a manifold is and provide some examples, but for now the reader that is not familiar with this concept can assume
M
is a sphere.)
From a theoretical perspective, one of the reasons why studying this field is important is that it generalizes unconstrained optimization to a class of spaces called Riemannian manifolds.
So, that means we can study and understand a wider number of problems because the Euclidean space is just one example of Riemannian manifold.
Another important reason is that this field can also be thought of as an alternative to constrained optimization as long as the constraints of the problem form a manifold.
A good example of this is, again, the sphere.
If one has an optimization problem given by

\text{minimize}\ \ f:\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}\ \ \text{subject to}\ \ \innerproduct{x}{x}=1,

(0.2)

then the constraint is actually a manifold and Riemannian Optimization provides mathematical tools to study this problem from a theoretical perspective (such as convergence guarantees) and algorithms to tackle it computationally.
The field is also important from a practical perspective because it increases the tool bag we can use to model, study and solve real-world problems.
That said, in the present work we will study a problem from Quantum Chemistry, but in
[
7
,
17
]
the reader can find other applications ranging from Chemistry and Physics to Computer Vision and Signal Processing.
Now let us motivate our problem.

In the late 1950s and early 1960s a medication called
Thalidomide
was marketed and prescribed to treat sleeping trouble and morning sickness in pregnant women.
However, when they started to take the drug, it passed across the placenta and harmed the developing fetus, leading to thousands of infants dying in the time of birth and many others surviving with debilitating malformations.
The cause of this unexpected effect was that Thalidomide existed inside the body in two different
molecular geometries
(i.e., the three-dimensional arrangement of the atoms that constitute a molecule) and, while one of the geometries helped the pregnant women, the other harmed the fetus
[
53
]
.
This is an extreme but very illustrative example of why computing and understanding molecular geometry is important.
Now, to compute the many possible geometries of a molecule and the energy necessary to change from one geometry to the other chemists created the concept of
Potential Energy Surface
(PES), which is a function that maps the geometries of a molecule to the energy that the molecule has in that geometry.
This function is important because the physically stable geometries are its minima and this is where the method we are going to study enters the picture.
Simplifying things a little bit, the function
F(\quantity{\vb{R}_i},\quantity{\phi_j(\vb{r}_j)})
that describes a PES depends on two sets of parameters: the arrangement of the atoms in space, which we denote by
\mathbf{R}_{i}
(each
\mathbf{R}_{i}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}
determines the position of the nucleus of the
i
-th atom after we fix an order for the atoms, for example), and the wave functions that describes the electrons orbiting these atoms, which we denote by
\phi_{j}(\mathbf{r}_{j})
(again,
\mathbf{r}_{j}\in\mathbf{R}^{3}
, but electrons are never fixed, this is why we need to consider a wave function instead of its “position”).
The optimization problem we are interested in is the
energy minimization
part, i.e., we want to find the wave functions that minimizes the energy once the nuclei are fixed.
This should not be confused with the problem of minimizing the whole function
F
, which is usually called
geometry optimization
in the literature.
In mathematical terms, we are interested in
currying
the function
F
and optimizing just
F(\quantity{\vb{R}_i},\cdot)
for a fixed geometry
\quantity{\vb{R}_i}
.
1
1

1

This is also known as a
parametric dependence
on the nuclei coordinates.

There are many ways to solve this problem of energy minimization and the
Hartree–Fock Method
(HF) is one of them.
We discuss this method in-depth in Chapter
3
, but for now let us just describe its importance and say that in this work we define HF as the optimization problem of energy minimization with the constraint that the wave function should be a
Slater determinant
.
2
2

2

Observe that the word
method
is a little misleading if we adopt this definition of Hartree–Fock because in this work method is usually meant as a synonym of
algorithm
. However, since in the literature Hartree–Fock is always referred as a method, we will also adopt this convention.

So, as already mentioned, there are multiple ways of modelling the problem of energy minimization and Hartree–Fock is the simplest method that takes into account wave functions, the Schrödinger equation and the antisymmetry of electrons.
These methods are usually called
ab initio
and they are the most accurate methods we have in Computational Quantum Chemistry nowadays, although the most expensive ones.
It is important to mention that Hartree–Fock is not the most accurate method for today’s standards, but many of the state-of-the-art methods aims to improve the Slater determinant obtained by HF (which is called
Hartree–Fock wave function
).
These methods are called
post-Hartree–Fock
and the fact that they use HF as a starting point shows why robust implementations of algorithms that solves HF are still highly desirable.
And this is where Riemannian Optimization enters the picture.

The Hartree–Fock Method, as the reader may have guessed, can be seen as a Riemannian optimization problem because the set of all Slater determinants is a manifold known as
Grassmann manifold
or, for short, the
Grassmannian
.
Consequently, we can use tools from Riemannian Optimization to tackle this problem and in the present work we implemented three Riemannian algorithms to solve HF: Gradient Descent (GD), Newton–Raphson (NR) and Conjugate Gradient (CG).
The main reason for this choice of algorithms is that they were all described in the seminal papers
[
17
,
45
]
, but the field has many other algorithms that can be used to solve HF and other problems, see
[
8
]
.
Now, one of the important aspects of using Riemannian Optimization to obtain a robust implementation for HF is that many algorithms with different strategies and complexities can be described using a common language.
This is good because we can, for example, use algorithms such as Gradient Descent or Conjugate Gradient when the starting point is too far (which usually means that the gradient of the cost function is big) and then switch to Newton–Raphson when the algorithm seems to be near convergence (i.e., the gradient is small and the Hessian is positive-definite).
In practice, combining CG with NR resulted in the best algorithm in terms of molecules converged (see Section
10
), but it should be pointed out that we did not analyze this and other heuristics systematically.

All the Riemannian methods previously described were implemented using the programming language Python and the reader can see it in the GitHub repository
[
5
]
.
We also implemented two other methods to compare with the Riemannian algorithms: a version of Newton–Raphson using Lagrange multipliers that can be found in any textbook on constrained optimization, and the Self-Consistent Field (SCF) method.
Among all algorithms implemented, the best one (without combining with others) in terms of molecules converged was CG, which converged for 93.2% of the molecules in the dataset.
The second best algorithm was SCF, which converged for 91.2% of the molecules.
Another interesting comparison we made was with respect to both Newton–Raphson methods.
The Riemannian Newton–Raphson converged for 83.8% of the molecules while the regular Newton–Raphson with Lagrange multipliers converged only for 53.4% of the molecules.
This is a very big improvement and it shows a real gain in using Riemannian algorithms, at least for Hartree–Fock.
With that, hopefully we convinced the reader why studying Hartre–Fock and Riemannian Optimization is worthwhile.

Now let us talk about the structure of this work.
It is organized as follows: in Chapter
2
we define all the mathematical objects necessary to implement the three Riemannian algorithms mentioned above and we also compute everything we need to implement these algorithms for the Grassmannian, which is the manifold we are mainly interested in this work.
In Chapter
3
we present the axioms and objects of Quantum Mechanics we need to understand the Hartree–Fock Method.
We also motivate this method from a physical and computational perspective.
Chapter
4
is where we define and write pseudocodes for the Riemannian algorithms already mentioned.
The pseudocodes were implemented for arbitrary cost functions defined in the product of two Grassmannians
3
3

3

We need two Grassmannians to take into account both spins of electrons, see Definition
3.10
.
, but we also filled the blank spaces with the cost function of the Hartree–Fock Method (i.e., the energy of a molecule) in Section
9
.
To conclude Chapter
4
, we present the results obtained and analyze them.
And last, but not least, in Chapter
5
we provide a conclusion for the present work and point towards what is next.
Now, the appendices.
The Topology and Linear Algebra appendices (
6
and
7
) are quite dry and they were written to set the set the language and contain the definitions and theorems we need in the main chapters.
In the Lagrange multipliers appendix (
8
) there is a pseudocode for the Newton–Raphson Method using Lagrange multipliers because we wanted to compare it with the Riemannian Newton–Raphson.
The result obtained by this algorithm is also in Section
10
.
To conclude, the Matrix identities appendix
9
provides some matrix identities and computations that are used in Chapter
4
and in the Lagrange multipliers appendix.

To conclude, a few remarks about the content of this work and how to actually read it.
One of the goals of this text is to be as self-contained as possible because different people with very distinct backgrounds will probably read it.
With that said, this work was written in such a way that the only prerequisite necessary to read it is Multivariable Calculus.
However, to be fair, the presentation of the fields involved is far from comprehensive, otherwise this text would have thousands of pages.
Also, knowing that fields such as Riemannian Geometry and Quantum Mechanics are not easy to grasp if the reader is encountering them for the first time in this work, the main chapters were written to be read independently and in a top down approach, which means it is possible to read the text starting from any chapter.
It is important to mention, though, that to achieve the independence of Chapter
4
we had to define and write some objects twice.
This was a deliberate choice.

2
Riemannian Geometry

Young man, in mathematics you don’t understand things. You just get used to them.

John von Neumann

1
Introduction

One of the goals of this chapter is to define and compute all the mathematical objects necessary to implement the Riemannian algorithms we will see in Chapter
4
.
Since the content of this chapter is, to a great extent, easily found in the literature, we opted for leaving most of the lemmas, propositions and theorems that are not directly related to the present work without proof.
Another goal of the present chapter is to show how Riemannian objects can be computed using the Grassmannian and the Stiefel manifold as examples because by doing this we hopefully provide a guide to the reader interested in working with other manifolds.
For a mathematical perspective of the subject containing all the proofs omitted, the references are
[
31
]
for the Differential Topology section and
[
32
]
for the Riemannian Geometry section.
However, the main reference used was
[
7
]
.

2
Differential Topology

The reader that is not familiar with Topology should read Appendix
6
first because we will use many definitions and results from Topology throughout this whole chapter.
Now, since all the fields involved in this chapter are quite advanced, a brief explanation of what they are all about is required.
Roughly speaking, the goal of Topology is to abstract the concept of space and study continuous deformations of these spaces.
However, sometimes the space has more structure than just a topology and this allows us to use tools from Linear Algebra and Calculus to study them.
That said, while pure mathematicians are interested in using these tools to study different kinds of deformations (smooth deformations in Differential Topology and isometries in Riemannian Geometry), in our case we are interested in these fields because they provide powerful tools to compute stuff and, as this whole work will show, computing objects such as gradients, Hessians, geodesics etc is quite useful to implement optimization algorithms, for example.
So, in essence the goal of Differential Topology and Riemannian Geometry is to provide a framework in which we can do Calculus and Linear Algebra on spaces that are a little more exotic than the Euclidean space and it is this framework that we will study in this chapter, with a focus on two “exotic” spaces called Stiefel manifold and Grassmannian.

Definition 2.1
.

A
d
-dimensional topological manifold
is a topological space that is second-countable, Hausdorff and locally Euclidean of dimension
d
.

Definition 2.2
.

Given a
d
-dimensional topological manifold
X
, a
chart of
X
is a pair
(U,\phi)
, being
U\subset X
an open set and
\phi:U\to V
a homeomorphism between
U
and an open subset
V
of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

Observation 2.3
.

We can replace the open set
V
in the definition above by
\mathbf{R}^{d}
itself or by an open ball
B(x,r)\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}
, but it is usually easier to find a homeomorphism with an arbitrary open set
V
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{m},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{n}
be arbitrary sets and
f:X_{1}\times\ldots\times X_{m}\to Y_{1}\times\ldots\times Y_{n}
be a function.
The
components of
f
are the composites
\pi_{i}\circ f
, being
\pi_{i}:Y_{1}\times\ldots\times Y_{n}\to Y_{i}
the canonical projection
\pi_{i}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})=y_{i}
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
U\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}
be an open subset and
f:U\to\mathbf{R}^{m}
be an arbitrary function.
We say that
f
is
smooth (in the Euclidean sense)
if all the partial derivatives of order
k
computed at
x
of all the component functions exist for every
k\in\mathbf{N}
and every
x\in U
.

Definition 2.6
.

Two charts
(U_{1},\phi_{1})
and
(U_{2},\phi_{2})
of
X
are said to be
compatible
if
U_{1}\cap U_{2}=\emptyset
or
\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}^{-1}:\phi_{2}(U_{1}\cap U_{2})\to\phi_{1}(U_{1}\cap U_{2})
is a diffeomorphism (i.e., a smooth bijection whose inverse is also smooth).

Definition 2.7
.

A set
\mathcal{A}=\quantity{(U_i, \phi_i) : i \in I}
of charts of
X
is said to be an
atlas for
X
if
X=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
and the charts are all compatible with each other.

Definition 2.8
.

Two atlases
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are said to be
compatible
if
\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
is again an atlas.

Definition 2.9
.

An atlas
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
maximal
if any other atlas that is compatible with
\mathcal{A}
is contained in
\mathcal{A}
.

Definition 2.10
.

A
(
d
-dimensional) smooth manifold
(also called
differentiable manifold
) is a pair
(X,\mathcal{A})
, being
X
a (
d
-dimensional) topological manifold and
\mathcal{A}
a maximal atlas.

Observation 2.11
.

When the atlas is not important, we will denote a smooth manifold just by
X
.

Observation 2.12
.

From now on all manifolds are assumed to be smooth.

Observation 2.13
.

It is always possible to extend an atlas
\mathcal{A}
to a unique maximal atlas
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
, just let
\overline{\mathcal{A}}
be the set of all charts of
X
that are compatible with all charts in
\mathcal{A}
.

This observation is very useful because we can now provide an infinite amount of examples of manifolds quite easily:

Example 2.14

(Open sets)
.

Any open subset
U
of the Euclidean space
\mathbf{R}^{d}
with the subspace topology and the atlas
\quantity{(U, \id_U)}
is a smooth manifold.
Consequently,
\mathbf{R}^{d}
is a manifold and open balls are manifolds.

Example 2.15

(Vector spaces)
.

Finite-dimensional vector spaces
X
over
\mathbf{R}
and
\mathbf{C}
(with the topology defined in Example
6.50
) are manifolds,
4
4

4

It is possible to generalize manifolds to cover the infinite-dimensional case, but we will not do so in the present work.
just consider the chart
\quantity{(X, T)}
, being
T:X\to\mathbf{R}^{d}
an isomorphism.
When the underlying field of the vector space is
\mathbf{C}
, we interpret
\mathbf{C}^{d}
as
\mathbf{R}^{2d}
.
It is important to mention that any other isomorphism
S:X\to\mathbf{R}^{d}
is compatible with
T
because
T\circ S^{-1}
is obviously a bijection and, since it can be represented by an invertible matrix, it is also a smooth function (its components are polynomials) and its inverse is smooth by the Inverse Function Theorem.

Example 2.16

(Symmetric matrices)
.

A particular example of vector space that will be useful later is
\sym(N)\coloneqq\quantity{M \in\mat{N}{N} : \text{$M$ is symmetric, \ie, $M = M^{\top}$}}
.
It is also important to mention that the topology on
\sym(N)
defined in Example
6.50
is the same as the subspace topology because this allows us to see
\sym(N)
as an embedded manifold inside
\text{Mat}(N\times N)
(we will define what embedded means in a moment, just keep that in mind).

Definition 2.17
.

A function between manifolds
f:X\to Y
is said to be
smooth at
x\in X
if there exists charts
(U_{X},\phi_{X})
and
(U_{Y},\phi_{Y})
of
X
and
Y
, respectively, such that
x\in U_{X}
,
f(U_{X})\subset U_{Y}
and
\phi_{Y}\circ f\circ\phi_{X}^{-1}:\phi_{X}(U_{X})\to\phi_{Y}(U_{Y})
is smooth in the Euclidean sense.
If
f
is smooth at every
x\in X
, then we say
f
is a
smooth function
.

Observation 2.18
.

We will denote the set of all smooth functions
f:X\to\mathbf{R}
by
C^{\infty}(X)
.

Now let us define the tangent space to a manifold at a fixed point.
Given
X
and
x\in X
, let

C_{x}\coloneqq\quantity{\gamma:(-\eps, \eps) \to X : \text{$\eps> 0$, $\gamma(0) = x$, $\gamma$ is smooth}}.

(2.1)

Consider the following equivalence relation in
C_{x}
: given a chart
(U,\phi)
and
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in C_{x}
, then
\gamma_{1}\sim_{\phi}\gamma_{2}
if, and only if,
(\phi\circ\gamma_{1})^{\prime}(0)=(\phi\circ\gamma_{2})^{\prime}(0)
.
This equivalence relation does not depend on the choice of chart, i.e., for any other chart
(\overline{U},\overline{\phi})
, we have
\gamma_{1}\sim_{\phi}\gamma_{2}
if, and only if,
\gamma_{1}\sim_{\overline{\phi}}\gamma_{2}
.
With that said, we will denote
\sim_{\phi}
just as
\sim
and we can define the tangent space:

Definition 2.19
.

Given a smooth manifold
X
and
x\in X
, the
tangent space (to
X
at
x
)
is the quotient
C_{x}/\sim
.
We will denote this quotient by
T_{x}M
and call its elements
tangent vectors
.

Observation 2.20
.

For any chart
(U,\phi)
containing
x\in X
it is possible to define the following bijection:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta_{x}^{\phi}:T_{x}M&\to\mathbf{R}^{d}\\
[\gamma]&\mapsto(\phi\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(0).\end{split}

(2.2)

Then, we induce a vector space structure on
T_{x}M
in the following way:

r[\gamma_{1}]+s[\gamma_{2}]\coloneqq(\theta_{x}^{\phi})^{-1}\quantity(r\theta_x^{\phi}([\gamma_1])
+ s\theta_x^{\phi}([\gamma_2])).

(2.3)

This structure does not depend on the choice of chart.

Example 2.21
.

If
X
is a finite-dimensional vector space, then
T_{x}X\cong X
for every
x\in X
.
The idea behind the proof of this example is that we can replace (equivalence classes of) curves passing through
x
by the derivative
\gamma^{\prime}(0)
because the linear structure of
X
allows us to define the derivative
\gamma^{\prime}
using limits as in Calculus.

Theorem 2.22

(Chain Rule)
.

If
f:X\to Y
and
g:Y\to Z
are smooth functions, then

T_{x}(g\circ f)=T_{f(x)}g\circ T_{x}f.

(2.4)

Definition 2.23
.

Given a smooth function
f:X\to Y
and
x\in X
, the
directional derivative (of
f
at
x
)
is the linear transformation defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{x}f:T_{p}X&\to T_{f(x)}Y\\
[\gamma]&\mapsto[f\circ\gamma].\end{split}

(2.5)

Example 2.24
.

When
X
and
Y
are vector spaces, the directional derivative can be computed as we learn in Calculus:

T_{x}f(v)=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{f(x+tv)-f(x)}{t}.

(2.6)

Observe that this only makes sense because
T_{x}X\cong X
and
T_{f(x)}Y\cong Y
.

Definition 2.25
.

Given a manifold
X
, its
tangent bundle
is the set

TX\coloneqq\bigcup_{x\in X}\quantity{x}\times T_{x}X=\quantity{(x, [\gamma]) : x \in X, \ [\gamma] \in T_xX}.

(2.7)

The tangent bundle comes with a natural function
\pi:TX\to X
, which we call
projection
, that is defined by
\pi(x,[\gamma])=x
.

Observation 2.26
.

The tangent bundle
TX
is actually a
2d
-dimensional manifold, being
d=\dim{X}
.
Its charts are constructed as follows: given a chart
(U,\phi)
of
X
, the charts of
TX
are
(\pi^{-1}(U),\widetilde{\phi})
, being
\pi^{-1}(U)
the preimage of
U
and
\widetilde{\phi}(x,[\gamma])\coloneqq(\phi(x),\theta_{x}^{\phi}([\gamma]))
.

Definition 2.27
.

A
vector field on
X
is a smooth function
V:X\to TX
such that
\pi\circ V=\id_{X}
.
The set of all vector fields on
X
will be denoted by
\mathfrak{X}(X)
.

Observation 2.28
.

Most of the time we will use
V(x)
or
Vx
to represent just the tangent vector instead of the pair
(x,V(x))
.

Observation 2.29
.

\mathfrak{X}(X)
is actually a vector space over
\mathbf{R}
and a module (a vector space over a ring instead of a field) over
C^{\infty}(X)
.
That means we can multiply
V\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
by a function
f:X\to\mathbf{R}
by doing
(fV)(x)\coloneqq f(x)V(x)
because
V(x)\in T_{x}X
,
f(x)\in\mathbf{R}
and
T_{x}X
is a vector space over
\mathbf{R}
.

Definition 2.30
.

A smooth function
f:X\to Y
is called an
immersion
if the directional derivative
T_{x}f:T_{x}X\to T_{f(x)}Y
is injective for every
x\in X
.

Definition 2.31
.

A smooth function
f:X\to Y
is called a
submersion
if the directional derivative
T_{x}f:T_{x}X\to T_{f(x)}Y
is surjective for every
x\in X
.

Example 2.32
.

The canonical inclusion
i:\sym(N)\to\text{Mat}(N\times N)
is an immersion because, since it is a linear transformation, we have
T_{M}i=i
for every
M\in\sym(N)
, which is obviously an injective function.

Definition 2.33
.

An immersion
f:X\to Y
is called an
embedding (of
X
into
Y
)
if
f
is also a topological embedding, i.e.,
X
and
f(X)
are homeomorphic when we endow
f(X)
with the subspace topology of
Y
.

Definition 2.34
.

An
embedded manifold
is a subset
S\subset X
such that
S
with the subspace topology and a maximal atlas for this topology makes the inclusion
i:S\to X
an embedding.

Example 2.35
.

\sym(N)
is embedded in
\text{Mat}(N\times N)
.

Observation 2.36
.

If
S
is embedded in
X
and
S^{\prime}
is embedded in
S
, then
S^{\prime}
is embedded in
X
.

Proposition 2.37
.

If
X
is embedded in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
(or any other vector space, as a matter of fact), then we can describe the tangent space more concretely as

T_{x}X=\quantity{\gamma'(0) \in\br^d : \text{$\gamma:(-\eps, \eps) \to X$ is smooth and $\gamma(0) = x$}}.

(2.8)

This is only possible because
T_{x}\mathbf{R}^{d}\cong\mathbf{R}^{d}
, though, since the definition of
\gamma^{\prime}(0)
is given by
\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\gamma(t)-\gamma(0)}{t}
, but subtracting
\gamma(t)
and
\gamma(0)
does not necessarily make sense if we consider just
X
.

Proposition 2.38
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a smooth function and
S\subset X
be an embedded manifold.
Then the restriction
f|_{S}:S\to Y
is also smooth.

Proposition 2.39
.

Let
X
be a smooth manifold and
S\subset X
be an embedded manifold.
Then every smooth function
f:Y\to X
whose image is contained in
S
is also smooth as a function from
Y
to
S
.

Observation 2.40
.

These propositions are extremely useful to prove that functions from and to embedded manifolds are smooth.
Keep them in mind throughout this whole chapter.

Definition 2.41
.

Given a smooth function
f:X\to Y
and
x\in X
, we say that
x
is a
regular point of
f
if
T_{x}f
is surjective.

Definition 2.42
.

Given a smooth function
f:X\to Y
and
y\in Y
, we say that
y
is a
regular value of
f
if every
x\in f^{-1}(y)
is a regular point of
f
.

Definition 2.43
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a smooth function and
S\subset X
be an embedded manifold such that
S=f^{-1}(y)
for some regular value
y\in Y
.
Then we say that
f
is a
defining function for
S
.

Proposition 2.44
.

Let
X
be a manifold and
S\subset X
be an embedded manifold.
If
f:X\to Y
is a defining function for
S
, then
T_{x}S=\ker{T_{x}f}
for every
x\in S
.

Theorem 2.45

(Preimage Theorem)
.

Given a smooth function
f:X\to Y
and a regular value
y\in Y
, its preimage
f^{-1}(y)
is an embedded manifold of dimension
\dim{X}-\dim{Y}
.

Observation 2.46
.

The previous theorem is also known as
Regular Level Set Theorem
.

This is one of the most useful and powerful theorems to show certain subsets of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
or
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
are manifolds.
Let us provide an example that is very important to the present work and which is actually three examples at the same time.

Example 2.47

(Stiefel manifold)
.

Given a symmetric and positive-definite matrix
S\in\text{Mat}(d\times d)
, consider the following set:

\text{St}(N,d)\coloneqq\quantity{C \in\mat{d}{N} : C^{\top}SC = \Id_N}.

(2.9)

Observe that when
N=d
and
S=\Id_{N}
, we have
\text{St}(N,N)=O(N)
, being
O(N)
the orthogonal group.
And when
S=\Id_{d}
and
N=1
, then
\text{St}(1,d)=\mathbf{S}^{d-1}
, being
\mathbf{S}^{d-1}
the
(d-1)
-dimensional sphere.
The set
\text{St}(N,d)
is called
(generalized) Stiefel manifold
and now let us prove that it is a manifold using the previous theorem.

Consider the following function (which is smooth because of Proposition
2.39
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f:\text{Mat}(d\times N)&\to\sym(N)\\
C&\mapsto C^{\top}SC-\Id_{N}.\end{split}

(2.10)

Observe that
\text{St}(N,d)=f^{-1}(0_{N})
.
Now, if we prove that
0_{N}
is a regular value of
f
, we have the desired result.
Let
C\in\text{St}(N,d)
.
Then, since these manifolds are vector spaces, the directional derivative is computed in the traditional way:

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{C}f(\mu)&=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{f(C+t\mu)-f(C)}{t}\\
&=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{(C+t\mu)^{\top}S(C+t\mu)-C^{\top}SC}{t}\\
&=\lim_{t\to 0}C^{\top}S\mu+\mu^{\top}SC+t\mu^{\top}S\mu\\
&=C^{\top}S\mu+\mu^{\top}SC.\end{split}

(2.11)

Now we need to show that this map is surjective.
Given
A\in\sym(N)
, if we let
\mu=\frac{1}{2}CA
, then

T_{C}f(\mu)=\frac{1}{2}C^{\top}SCA+\frac{1}{2}A^{\top}C^{\top}SC=\frac{1}{2}A+\frac{1}{2}A^{\top}=A

(2.12)

because
A=A^{\top}
and
C^{\top}SC=\Id_{N}
.
The result follows.
To conclude, let us compute its tangent space.
Since
f
is a defining function for the Stiefel manifold, Proposition
2.44
tells us that

T_{C}\text{St}(N,d)=\ker{T_{C}f}=\quantity{\mu\in\mat{d}{N} : C^{\top}S\mu+ \mu^{\top}SC = 0_N}.

(2.13)

Proposition 2.48
.

The Stiefel manifold
\text{St}(N,d)
is compact.

Proof.

According to the Heine–Borel Theorem (
6.77
), we need to prove that
\text{St}(N,d)
is a closed and bounded subset of
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
.
Now, we will consider in
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
the topology induced by the inner product
\innerproduct{C_1}{C_2}\coloneqq\tr(C_1^{\top}SC_2)
, but, since all norms in
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
are equivalent (for a proof, see
[
11
]
), the topology induced is the same and Heine–Borel still holds in this case.
With that said, since the defining function
f
for the Stiefel manifold is continuous and since
\text{St}(N,d)=f^{-1}(0_{N})
, we already have that the Stiefel is closed.
To show that it is bounded, observe that, if
C\in\text{St}(N,d)
, then
\norm{C}^{2}=\tr(C^{\top}SC)=\tr(\Id_N)=N
.
Consequently, the Stiefel manifold is contained in the ball
B(0,\sqrt{N}+\varepsilon)
for any
\varepsilon>0
, which means it is bounded.
The result follows.
∎

Example 2.49
.

Given
k
manifolds
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
, we can define the
product manifold (of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
)
as the set
X_{1}\times\ldots\times X_{k}
with the product topology (see Definition
6.42
) and with the charts constructed as follows: given charts
(U_{i},\phi_{i})
of
X_{i}
, a chart of
X_{1}\times\ldots\times X_{k}
is defined by
(U_{1}\times\ldots\times U_{k},\phi_{1}\times\ldots\times\phi_{k})
, being
(\phi_{1}\times\ldots\times\phi_{k})(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\coloneqq(\phi_{1}(x_{1}),\ldots,\phi_{k}(x_{k}))
.

Observation 2.50
.

From now on when we consider the product of two or more manifolds, the smooth structure we will consider is always the one described above.

Proposition 2.51
.

If
X_{1},\ldots,X_{k}
are manifolds, then

T_{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})}(X_{1}\times\ldots\times X_{k})\cong T_{x_{1}}X_{1}\times\ldots\times T_{x_{k}}X_{k}

(2.14)

because the function
[\gamma]\mapsto([\pi_{1}\circ\gamma],\ldots,[\pi_{k}\circ\gamma])
, being
\pi_{i}
the canonical projection, is an isomorphism.

Definition 2.52
.

A smooth manifold
G
that is also a group (see Definition
7.1
) is called a
Lie group
if the group operation
m:G\times G\to G
and the inverse
(\cdot)^{-1}:G\to G
are smooth functions.

Example 2.53
.

The orthogonal group
O(N)
is a Lie group because the product of matrices and the inverse of a matrix are smooth functions since the component functions are polynomials (for the inverse recall Cramer’s rule).
Observe that we are using Propositions
2.38
and
2.39
because
O(N)
is an embedded manifold.

Definition 2.54
.

An
action
of a group
G
on a set
X
is a map
\theta:X\times G\to X
such that
\theta(x,e)=x
and
\theta(\theta(x,g),h)=\theta(x,gh)
for every
x\in X
and
g,h\in G
.
Recall that
e\in G
represents the identity of the group.

Observation 2.55
.

The definition above is actually called a
right action
, but we will call it just action because we will not use left actions in this work.

Example 2.56
.

Let
X=\text{St}(N,d)
and
G=O(N)
.
The function

\displaystyle\begin{split}\theta:\text{St}(N,d)\times O(N)&\to\text{St}(N,d)\\
(C,M)&\mapsto CM\end{split}

(2.15)

defines an action of
O(N)
on
\text{St}(N,d)
.

Definition 2.57
.

Given a set
X
,
x\in X
and an action
\theta:X\times G\to X
, we define the
stabilizer of
x
to be the set
\stab(x)\coloneqq\quantity{g \in G : \theta(x, g) = x}
.

Definition 2.58
.

A group action is called
free
if
\stab(x)=\quantity{e}
for every
x\in X
.

Example 2.59
.

The action defined in Example
2.56
is free.
Indeed,
CM=C
if, and only if,
C^{\top}SCM=C^{\top}SC
.
Therefore, since
C^{\top}SC=\Id_{N}
, we have
M=\Id_{N}
and the result follows.

Definition 2.60
.

Given a set
X
,
x\in X
and an action
\theta:X\times G\to X
, we define the
orbit of
x
to be the set
\orb(x)\coloneqq\quantity{\theta(x, g) : g \in G}
.

Definition 2.61
.

If we have a group
G
acting on
X
, it is possible to define an equivalence relation on
X
as follows:
x\sim y
if, and only if,
y\in\orb(x)
.
The quotient
X/\sim
will be denoted by
X/G
and we will call it
orbit space
.

Definition 2.62
.

Given a manifold
X
and a Lie group
G
acting on
X
, we say that the action
\theta
is
smooth
if the function
\theta:X\times G\to X
is smooth.

Example 2.63
.

The action of Example
2.56
is smooth because multiplying matrices is smooth (again, recall that the manifolds are embedded).

Definition 2.64
.

Given a manifold
X
and a Lie group
G
acting on
X
, we say that the action
\theta
is
proper
if the function
\id_{X}\times\theta:X\times G\to X\times X
given by
(\id_{X}\times\theta)(p,g)=(p,\theta(p,g))
is proper.

Proposition 2.65
.

Every continuous action by a compact Lie group on a manifold is proper.

Corollary 2.66
.

Since we already showed that the Stiefel manifold is compact and that the orthogonal group is just a particular case of a Stiefel manifold, the previous proposition shows that the action defined in
2.56
is proper.

Theorem 2.67

(Quotient Manifold Theorem)
.

Suppose
G
is a Lie group acting smoothly, freely and properly on a smooth manifold
X
.
Then the orbit space
X/G
admits a unique smooth structure such that the canonical projection
\pi:X\to X/G
is a submersion.
Also,
\dim{X/G}=\dim{X}-\dim{G}
.

Observation 2.68
.

We are considering the quotient topology (Example
6.34
) on
X/G
.

Example 2.69

(Grassmannian)
.

According to the previous theorem (and from what we have showed previously), the quotient
\text{St}(N,d)/O(N)
is a manifold and we will call it
(generalized) Grassmannian
or
(generalized) Grassmann manifold
.
The generalized part is because in the definition of Stiefel manifold we are considering matrices
C\in\text{Mat}(d\times N)
such that
C^{\top}SC=\Id_{N}
, being
S\in\text{Mat}(d\times d)
a symmetric and positive-definite matrix.

The above definition is the one we will use in Chapter
4
, but the most popular definition of Grassmannian and the one we will use in Chapter
3
is the following: let
X
be a
d
-dimensional vector space.
Then, given
0\leq N\leq d
, the Grassmannian can also be defined as
\text{Gr}(N,X)\coloneqq\quantity{L \subset X : \text{$L$ is a subspace of $X$ and $\dim{L} = N$}}
.
Now, it is worth mentioning how we can move from one definition to the other.
If we start with an equivalence class
[C]\in\text{St}(N,d)/O(N)
, then to obtain a subspace of dimension
N
we just need to consider the subspace spanned by the columns of
C
.
On the other hand, if we start with a subspace
L\subset X
, then we need first to choose a basis
B=\quantity{v_1, \ldots, v_d}
for
X
such that
S=\quantity(\braket{v_i}{v_j})_{i,j}
.
Secondly, we choose an orthonormal basis for
L
:
B_{L}=\quantity{\l_1, \ldots, \l_N}
.
Then, we write the vectors of
B_{L}
as linear combinations of the vectors in
B
:
\ell_{j}=c_{1j}v_{1}+\ldots+c_{dj}v_{d}
.
And, to conclude, we consider the equivalence class
[C]
, being
C=\quantity(c_{ij})_{i,j}
.
Observe that this matrix
C
satisfies
C^{\top}SC=\Id_{N}
because we assumed
B_{L}
to be orthonormal, even if the basis
B
is not (which gives rise to the matrix
S
).

Example 2.70

(Projective space)
.

The manifold
\text{Gr}(1,d)
or, more generally,
\text{Gr}(1,X)
, is also known as
projective space
and it is often denoted by
\mathbf{P}{X}
.

Observation 2.71
.

We will usually say that the Grassmannian defined as a quotient of the Stiefel manifold is the
concrete Grassmannian
and the definition as a set of subspaces is the
abstract Grassmannian
.

Observation 2.72
.

Observe that the abstract definition of the Grassmannian also works for the case in which
X
is infinite-dimensional (always assuming
N\in\mathbf{N}
), but it is not possible to establish the correspondence with the quotient of the Stiefel manifold anymore, at least not as we defined the Stiefel (there is also an abstract version of this manifold, see
[
2
]
).
It is important to mention this because in Quantum Mechanics (Chapter
3
) we will usually deal with the projective space of infinite-dimensional vector spaces and the definition we are considering there is the abstract one.
However, we will not use the (infinite-dimensional) manifold structure of these spaces.

Lemma 2.73
.

Given a manifold
X
and a Lie group
G
acting on
X
, the canonical projection
\pi:X\to X/G
is an open map.

Theorem 2.74
.

If
X
is a compact manifold and
G
is a Lie group acting on
X
, then
X/G
is compact.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{U}
is a cover of
X/G
, then, since the projection is continuous,
\quantity{\pi^{-1}(U) : U \in\mc{U}}
is a cover of
X
.
Now, using the compactness of
X
, there exists
U_{1},\ldots,U_{k}\in\mathcal{U}
such that
X=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\pi^{-1}(U_{i})
.
However, if we use the previous lemma and the surjectivity of
\pi
, we know that
\pi(\pi^{-1}(U_{i}))=U_{i}
are open in
X/G
.
That is it,
X/G=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}
because
\pi
is surjective.
∎

Corollary 2.75
.

The Grassmannian is compact.

Proof.

Using the fact that the Stiefel manifold is compact and the previous theorem the result follows.
∎

Corollary 2.76
.

Finite products of the Grassmannian and of the Stiefel manifold are compact.

Proof.

It follows from the fact that the Grassmannian and the Stiefel manifold are compact together with the fact that finite products of compact spaces are compact (Proposition
6.67
).
∎

Definition 2.77
.

Given a manifold
X
and an equivalence relation
\sim
, we say that the quotient
X/\sim
is a
quotient manifold
if it has a smooth structure such that the canonical projection
\pi:X\to X/\sim
is a submersion.

Observation 2.78
.

Theorem
2.67
is basically saying that
X/G
is a quotient manifold.

Now, there is a very useful result that allows us to obtain smooth functions defined on quotient manifolds (and, consequently, on the Grassmannian):

Theorem 2.79
.

Given a quotient manifold
X/\sim
, a function
f:X/\sim\to Y
is smooth if, and only if,
f\circ\pi:X\to Y
is smooth, being
\pi:X\to X/\sim
the canonical projection.

The next theorem is useful to find the tangent space to a quotient manifold, which is necessary to do Riemannian Geometry:

Theorem 2.80
.

Given a quotient manifold
X/\sim
and
x\in X
, the set
\pi^{-1}(\pi(x))
is called
fiber of
x
and (1) it is a closed set of
X
, (2) it is an embedded manifold of
X
, (3) its tangent spaces are given by

T_{y}\pi^{-1}(\pi(x))=\ker{T_{y}\pi},

(2.16)

being
y\in\pi^{-1}(\pi(x))
and
\pi
the canonical projection.
When
y=x
, the tangent space is called
vertical space
and it is denoted by
V_{x}
.

Observation 2.81
.

Now, since
T_{x}\pi:T_{x}X\to T_{[x]}(X/\sim)
is a surjective linear transformation, we can use the Universal Property of the Quotient (Proposition
7.32
) to obtain
T_{[x]}(X/\sim)\cong T_{x}X/V_{x}
, which means we can compute the tangent space to the quotient manifold using
X
and
\pi
.
This is already useful, but when the manifold is Riemannian we can describe this quotient more concretely.
So, let us move to Riemannian manifolds.

3
Riemannian Geometry

Again, all manifolds are assumed to be smooth.
Also, since in this section we will use tangent vectors multiple times, specially in quotient manifolds, we will switch the notation from
[\gamma]
to
v
to improve readability.

Definition 2.82
.

Given a manifold
X
, a
(Riemannian) metric on
X
is a function
x\mapsto\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot}_{x}
that chooses an inner product on
T_{x}X
smoothly, i.e.,
\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot}_{x}:T_{x}X\times T_{x}X\to\mathbf{R}
is an inner product and, given two vector fields
V_{1},V_{2}
on
X
, the function
X\ni x\mapsto\innerproduct{V_1(x)}{V_2(x)}_{x}\in\mathbf{R}
should be smooth.
A manifold endowed with a Riemannian metric is called a
Riemannian manifold
and we will usually denote the Riemannian metric by
g
or
\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot}
.

Observation 2.83
.

We are slightly abusing the notation in the definition above because
V_{i}(x)
is actually representing a vector in
T_{x}X
instead of a pair
(x,V_{i}(x))
in
TX
.

Observation 2.84
.

Given the fact that we have an inner product, we can induce a norm in each tangent space in the natural way:
\norm{v}_{x}=\sqrt{\innerproduct{v}{v}_{x}}
, being
v\in T_{x}X
.

Observation 2.85
.

When the metric does not depend explicitly on
x
, as we will see in the next example, we will drop the subscript and denote
\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot}_{x}
by
\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot}
.

Example 2.86

(Vector spaces)
.

Any vector space endowed with an inner product
(X,\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot})
is a Riemannian manifold because, since
T_{x}X\cong X
for every
x\in X
, we can use
\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot}
also as a Riemannian metric.
Observe that in this case the inner product is the same for the manifold and all its tangent spaces.
Therefore, we will ignore the subscript.

Example 2.87

(Euclidean space)
.

When
X=\mathbf{R}^{d}
, we will always consider the inner product given by
\innerproduct{u}{v}=u^{\top}v
.

Example 2.88

(Matrix space)
.

Observe that, if
X=\text{Mat}(d\times N)
, then
\innerproduct{M_1}{M_2}=\tr(M_1^{\top}M_2)=\vecc(M_{1})^{\top}\vecc(M_{2})
, being vec the columnwise vectorization (see Definition
9.6
).
So, the inner product in the matrix space
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
and in the Euclidean space
\mathbf{R}^{dN}
is actually the same if we adopt this columnwise convention.
That said, we will mostly use the following inner product when we are dealing with matrices:
\innerproduct{M_1}{M_2}=\tr(M_1^{\top}SM_2)
, being
S\in\text{Mat}(d\times d)
a symmetric and positive-definite matrix.

Example 2.89
.

If
(X,g^{X})
and
(Y,g^{Y})
are Riemannian manifolds, then the product
X\times Y
is also a Riemannian manifold and the metric is given by:

g_{(x,y)}^{X\times Y}((v_{x},v_{y}),(w_{x},w_{y}))\coloneqq g_{x}^{X}(v_{x},w_{x})+g_{y}^{Y}(v_{y},w_{y}).

(3.1)

Example 2.90

(Embedded manifolds)
.

If
f:X\to Y
is an immersion and
(Y,g^{Y})
is a Riemannian manifold, we can define a metric on
X
as follows:

g_{x}^{X}(v,w)\coloneqq g_{f(x)}^{Y}(T_{x}f(v),T_{x}f(w)).

(3.2)

This metric
g^{X}
is usually called
pullback metric (of
g^{Y}
along
f
)
and it is denoted by
f^{*}g^{Y}
.

Definition 2.91
.

If
(X,g^{X})
is a Riemannian manifold and
S\subset X
is an embedded manifold, then
S
endowed with the pullback metric along the inclusion is called a
Riemannian submanifold (of
X
)
.

Example 2.92

(Stiefel manifold)
.

We already showed that the Stiefel manifold
\text{St}(N,d)
is embedded in
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
and we also computed its tangent spaces in Example
2.47
.
So, if we consider the pullback metric on the Stiefel, we have a more “exotic” example of Riemannian manifold.
That said, since we are working with a generalized Stiefel, we have to fix the inner product a little bit.
So, to us, the inner product on
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
and
\text{St}(N,d)
is given by
\innerproduct{M_1}{M_2}\coloneqq\tr(M_1^{\top}SM_2)
, being
S
the symmetric and positive-definite matrix underlying the generalized Stiefel.
Some people claim that this metric is not the best one to consider in the Stiefel manifold and this makes a big difference when one is implementing Riemannian algorithms.
However, since we will implement algorithms on the Grassmannian using the Stiefel just as a helper, we will keep using this metric.
If the reader is more interested in the Stiefel manifold, though, check
[
17
]
to see a discussion about the metric issue and for algorithms.

In Observation
2.81
we said that the tangent space to a quotient manifold
X/\sim
is isomorphic to
T_{x}X/V_{x}
, being
V_{x}=\ker{T_{x}\pi}
and
\pi
the canonical projection.
However, when
X
is a Riemannian manifold,
T_{x}X/V_{x}
is isomorphic to the orthogonal complement of
V_{x}
and this space is easier to work with.
5
5

5

The isomorphism is obtained by considering the orthogonal projection
\pi:T_{x}X\to V_{x}^{\perp}
and then using the Universal Property of the Quotient (
7.32
).

Definition 2.93
.

The orthogonal complement of
V_{x}
is called
horizontal space
and we will denote it by
H_{x}
.
It is worth emphasizing that
H_{x}\cong T_{[x]}(X/\sim)
.

Observation 2.94
.

H_{x}\cong H_{y}
for every
x,y\in X
such that
x\sim y
.

Definition 2.95
.

Given a quotient manifold
X/\sim
,
[x]\in X/\sim
and
v\in T_{[x]}(X/\sim)
, we define the
horizontal lift of
v
to be the unique vector
\lift_{x}(v)\in H_{x}
such that
T_{x}\pi(\lift_{x}(v))=v
.
The uniqueness is given by the fact that
H_{x}
is isomorphic to
T_{[x]}(X/\sim)
.
Also, if
V\in\mathfrak{X}(X/\sim)
is a vector field in the quotient, then the
horizontal lift of
V
, which we will denote by
\overline{V}
, is the vector field
\overline{V}\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
such that
\overline{V}(x)=\lift_{x}(V([x]))
.

Definition 2.96
.

If
(X,\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot}^{X})
is a Riemannian manifold, then a quotient manifold
X/\sim
with the metric
\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot}^{X/\sim}
defined by

\innerproduct{v}{w}_{[x]}^{X/\sim}\coloneqq\innerproduct{\lift_x(v)}{\lift_x(w)}_{x}^{X}

(3.3)

is called a
Riemannian quotient manifold
.
However, this metric is well-defined only if

\innerproduct{\lift_x(v)}{\lift_x(w)}_{x}^{X}=\innerproduct{\lift_y(v)}{\lift_y(w)}_{y}^{X}

(3.4)

for every
y\in X
such that
y\sim x
.

Example 2.97

(Grassmannian)
.

Changing the notation from
x
to
C
, let us compute the vertical space
V_{C}
first.
Recall that the fiber
\pi^{-1}(\pi(C))
is an embedded manifold of
\text{St}(N,d)
(Quotient Manifold Theorem
2.67
).
Therefore, we can compute its tangent space (which is, by definition,
V_{C}
) in the usual way, i.e., we see
\pi^{-1}(\pi(C))
as an embedded manifold of
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
(Observation
2.36
) and then consider the derivative of a curve
\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\pi^{-1}(\pi(C))
computed at
0
(Proposition
2.37
).
Now, since the Grassmannian is defined as
\text{St}(N,d)/O(N)
(Example
2.69
), it is not hard to see that
\pi^{-1}(\pi(C))=C\cdot O(N)\coloneqq\quantity{CM : M \in O(N)}
.
Consequently, the curve
\gamma
can be written as
\gamma(t)=CM(t)
, being
M(t)\in O(N)
for every
t\in(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
.
Besides, since we also know that
\gamma(0)=C
, we have
M(0)=\Id_{N}
.
So, this tells us that
T_{C}\pi^{-1}(\pi(C))\cong C\cdot T_{\Id_{N}}O(N)
.
Recalling that
T_{\Id_{N}}O(N)=\quantity{\nu\in\mat{N}{N} : \nu= -\nu^{\top}}
(this is a particular case of the Stiefel manifold and we already computed the tangent space to the Stiefel manifold at Example
2.47
), we have

V_{C}=T_{C}\pi^{-1}(\pi(C))\cong C\cdot T_{\Id_{N}}O(N)=\quantity{C\nu\in\mat{d}{N} : \nu= -\nu^{\top}}

(3.5)

and

H_{C}=\quantity{\eta\in T_C\st{N}{d} : \text{$\braket{C\nu}{\eta} = \tr(\nu^{\top}C^{\top}S\eta) = 0$ for every $C\nu\in V_C$}}.

(3.6)

However, if
\tr(\nu^{\top}C^{\top}S\eta)=0
for every
\nu
antisymmetric, then
C^{\top}S\eta
is a symmetric matrix because, if we choose
\nu=E_{ij}-E_{ij}^{\top}
(
E_{ij}
is defined in
9.1
), then
\tr(E_{ij}^{\top}C^{\top}S\eta)-\tr(E_{ij}C^{\top}S\eta)=(C^{\top}S\eta)_{ij}-(C^{\top}S\eta)_{ji}=0
.
On the other hand,
\eta\in T_{C}\text{St}(N,d)
means, by definition, that
C^{\top}S\eta
is antisymmetric.
Consequently, since the only matrix that is symmetric and antisymmetric at the same time is
0
, we have

H_{C}=\quantity{\eta\in\mat{d}{N} : C^{\top}S\eta= 0_N}.

(3.7)

To conclude we have to verify that Equation
3.4
is satisfied in the Grassmannian, which is the same as proving that

\innerproduct{\lift_C(\eta_1)}{\lift_C(\eta_2)}_{C}^{\St}=\innerproduct{\lift_{CM}(\eta_1)}{\lift_{CM}(\eta_2)}_{CM}^{\St}

(3.8)

for every
M\in O(N)
because
[C]=\quantity{CM : M \in O(N)}
.
Now, given
M\in O(N)
and
\eta\in T_{[C]}\text{Gr}(N,d)
, let
\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\text{St}(N,d)
be a curve such that
\gamma(0)=C
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=\lift_{C}(\eta)
.
Consider also the curve
\rho:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\text{St}(N,d)
given by
\rho(t)=\gamma(t)M
.
Then we have that
\rho(0)=CM
and
\rho^{\prime}(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(0)M=\lift_{C}(\eta)M
.
Now, according to the Chain Rule (Theorem
2.22
), we have
(\pi\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(0)=T_{\gamma(0)}\pi(\gamma^{\prime}(0))=T_{C}\pi(\lift_{C}(\eta))=\eta
.
However, since
\pi\circ\gamma
and
\pi\circ\rho
are the same curve on
\text{Gr}(N,d)
, we can conclude that

\eta=(\pi\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(0)=(\pi\circ\rho)^{\prime}(0)=T_{\rho(0)}\pi(\rho^{\prime}(0))=T_{CM}\pi(\lift_{C}(\eta)M).

(3.9)

Then, using the uniqueness of the horizontal lift we conclude that

\lift_{CM}(\eta)=\lift_{C}(\eta)M.

(3.10)

Finally, using the fact that
\tr(A_1A_2A_3A_4A_5)=\tr(A_5A_1A_2A_3A_4)
and the identity above, we can show that Equation
3.8
is valid:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\innerproduct{\lift_{CM}(\eta_1)}{\lift_{CM}(\eta_2)}_{CM}^{\St}&=\innerproduct{\lift_C(\eta_1)M}{\lift_C(\eta_2)M}_{CM}^{\St}\\
&=\tr(M^{\top}\lift_C(\eta_1)^{\top}S\lift_C(\eta_2)M)\\
&=\tr(\lift_C(\eta_1)^{\top}S\lift_C(\eta_2))\\
&=\innerproduct{\lift_C(\eta_1)}{\lift_C(\eta_2)}_{C}^{\St}.\end{split}

(3.11)

Consequently, the Grassmannian is indeed a Riemannian quotient manifold of the Stiefel.
That is it, from now on we can ignore the abstract definition of
T_{[C]}\text{Gr}(N,d)
and work only with
H_{C}
.

Definition 2.98
.

Given a Riemannian manifold
(X,\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot})
and a smooth function
f:X\to\mathbf{R}
, the
(Riemannian) gradient of
f
is the unique vector field
\gradd{f}:X\to TX
that satisfies

\innerproduct{v}{\grad{f}(x)}_{x}=T_{x}f(v)

(3.12)

for every
v\in T_{x}X
.

Example 2.99
.

When
X
is a vector space, we can choose an orthonormal basis
B=\quantity{v_1, \ldots, v_d}
for
X
and then a concrete description for the gradient is given by the directional derivatives:

\gradd{f}(x)=\partialderivative{f}{v_1}\quantity(x)v_{1}+\ldots+\partialderivative{f}{v_d}\quantity(x)v_{d},

(3.13)

being

\partialderivative{f}{v_i}\quantity(x)\coloneqq\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{f(x+tv_{i})-f(x)}{t}.

(3.14)

Observe that when
X=\mathbf{R}^{d}
and we choose the canonical basis
\quantity{e_1, \ldots, e_d}
, we obtain the gradient from Calculus.
It is also worth mentioning that if
X=\text{Mat}(d\times N)
, then the gradient can also be written as a matrix whose entry
(i,j)
is
\partialderivative{f}{v_{ij}}\quantity(x)
.

Example 2.100
.

Now let us see how we can change the gradient from one basis to another because we will need this for the Grassmannian and the Stiefel.
Suppose we have a vector space
X
and two basis for it:
B_{v}=\quantity{v_1, \ldots, v_d}
and
B_{w}=\quantity{w_1, \ldots, w_d}
.
If we write
w_{j}=a_{1j}v_{1}+\ldots+a_{dj}v_{d}
and assume
B_{v}
is orthonormal, then
a_{ij}=\innerproduct{v_i}{w_j}
and we can build the matrix
A=\quantity(a_{ij})_{i,j}
.
Also, if we write
A^{-1}=\quantity(a^{ij})_{i,j}
, then
v_{j}=a^{1j}w_{1}+\ldots+a^{dj}w_{d}
.
Now, using the linearity of the directional derivative, we have:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gradd{f}(x)&=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partialderivative{f}{v_k}\quantity(x)v_{k}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\partialderivative{f}{\sum_{i=1}^d a^{ik}w_i}\quantity(x)\cdot\quantity(\sum_{j=1}^d a^{jk}w_j)\\
&=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{d}a^{ik}a^{jk}\partialderivative{f}{w_i}\quantity(x)w_{j}.\end{split}

(3.15)

However, if we use the following identity

\innerproduct{w_i}{w_j}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\innerproduct{\braket{v_k}{w_i}v_k}{w_j}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\innerproduct{v_k}{w_i}\innerproduct{v_k}{w_j}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}a_{ki}a_{kj},

(3.16)

then the Gram matrix
G_{w}\coloneqq\quantity(\braket{w_i}{w_j})_{i,j}
is equal to
A^{\top}A
.
Consequently,
G_{w}^{-1}=(A^{\top}A)^{-1}=A^{-1}A^{-\top}
and that means the gradient in the basis
B_{w}
is given by:

\gradd{f}(x)=G_{w}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\partialderivative{f}{w_1}\quantity(x)\\
\vdots\\
\partialderivative{f}{w_d}\quantity(x)\end{bmatrix}_{B_{w}},

(3.17)

where the subscript
B_{w}
means we are writing the vector in the basis
B_{w}
.
Observe that this also works for the case in which
X=\text{Mat}(d\times N)
, i.e., we have
G_{w}^{-1}
multiplying the matrix
\quantity(\pdv{f}{w_{ij}}\qty(x))_{i,j}
.

Proposition 2.101
.

Given a smooth function
f:X\times Y\to\mathbf{R}
, the Riemannian gradient on this product is given by

\gradd{f}(x,y)=\quantity(\grad{f}(\cdot, y)(x), \grad{f}(x, \cdot)(y)).

(3.18)

Proposition 2.102
.

Let
X
be a Riemannian submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
,
f:X\to\mathbf{R}
be a smooth function and
\overline{f}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{R}
be an extension of
f
.
Then, the Riemannian gradient of
f
is given by:

\gradd{f}(x)=\proj_{x}^{X}(\gradd{\overline{f}}(x)),

(3.19)

being
\proj_{x}^{X}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{R}^{d}
the orthogonal projection to
T_{x}X
.

Observation 2.103
.

The idea behind this result is: compute the gradient in the traditional way by considering an extension and then project the result.
In other words, the intuition is that the Riemannian gradient is the projection of the Euclidean gradient.
Sometimes, as we will see in Section
9
,
\overline{f}
and
f
have the same expression.

Example 2.104
.

When
X=\text{St}(N,d)
, the orthogonal projection is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\proj_{C}^{\St}:\text{Mat}(d\times N)&\to\text{Mat}(d\times N)\\
\mu&\mapsto(\Id_{d}-CC^{\top}S)\mu+\frac{CC^{\top}S\mu-C\mu^{\top}SC}{2}\end{split}

(3.20)

and the reader can find a full derivation of this projection in
[
7
, Section 7.3]
.
Now, computing the gradient is a little trickier because we are working with the generalized Stiefel.
Basically, in our case the Stiefel is built in the following way: we start with a non-orthogonal basis
B_{v}=\quantity{v_1, \ldots, v_d}
of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
and then we extend this to a basis of
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
by considering
v_{ij}\coloneqq\quantity(0, \ldots, v_i, \ldots, 0)
, being
v_{i}
located in the
j
-th entry.
We also have that
S=\quantity(v_i^{\top}v_j)_{i,j}
, but in this case we are considering
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
as linear combinations of the canonical basis of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
With all that said, to compute the gradient of a function
f:\text{St}(N,d)\to\mathbf{R}
, we need to extend it to
\overline{f}:\text{Mat}(d\times N)\to\mathbf{R}
and then recall what we have discussed in Examples
2.99
and
2.100
to obtain the following gradient for a function defined in the Stiefel:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gradd{f}(C)&=\proj_{C}^{\St}(\gradd{\overline{f}}(C))\\
&=(\Id_{d}-CC^{\top}S)S^{-1}\gradd{\overline{f}}(C)+\frac{CC^{\top}SS^{-1}\gradd{\overline{f}}(C)-C\gradd{\overline{f}}(C)^{\top}S^{-1}SC}{2}\\
&=(\Id_{d}-CC^{\top}S)S^{-1}\gradd{\overline{f}}(C)+\frac{CC^{\top}\gradd{\overline{f}}(C)-C\gradd{\overline{f}}(C)^{\top}C}{2}.\end{split}

(3.21)

So, the intuition is: compute the gradient of
\overline{f}
as we learn in Calculus.
Fix it multiplying by
S^{-1}
on the left to get back to the non-orthogonal basis we started with.
To conclude, project the result to obtain a tangent vector to the Stiefel.

If the reader is alert for the details, then using the matrix
S^{-1}
may seem suspicious because the matrix
S
is the Gram matrix for
\mathbf{R}^{d}
and not for
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
, which is what we need.
However, since the basis we are considering for
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
is built using a basis of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
as we explained previously, the Gram matrix for
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
is
S\otimes\Id_{N}
, being
\otimes
the Kronecker product (see Definition
9.5
).
As the reader can notice, the dimension now is correct, but to use
S\otimes\Id_{N}
we would need to vectorize the matrices from
\text{Mat}(d\times N)
.
Now, a very fortunate result says that we can keep using just
S
because, given
C\in\text{Mat}(d\times N)
, we have
(S\otimes\Id_{N})\vecc(C)=SC
(see Appendix
9
).

Proposition 2.105
.

Let
X/\sim
be a Riemannian quotient manifold,
X
be embedded in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
and
f:X/\sim\to\mathbf{R}
be a smooth function.
Then, the Riemannian gradient of
f
is given by:

\gradd{f}([x])=\proj_{x}^{\hor}(\gradd{\overline{\overline{f}}}(x)),

(3.22)

being
\overline{\overline{f}}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{R}
a smooth extension of
\overline{f}:X\to\mathbf{R}
;
\overline{f}
a lift of
f
, i.e.,
\overline{f}=f\circ\pi
; and
\proj_{x}^{\hor}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{R}^{d}
the orthogonal projection to
H_{x}
.

Observation 2.106
.

Again, the main idea is finding a way to use the regular Euclidean gradient, since this gradient we already know how to compute.
In this case the easiest way requires three steps, though: lifting the original function, extending the lift and then projecting the Euclidean gradient to the horizontal space because this is the space tangent to the quotient manifold.

Example 2.107
.

For the Grassmannian, the projection to the horizontal space is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\proj_{[C]}^{\hor}:\text{Mat}(d\times N)&\to\text{Mat}(d\times N)\\
\eta&\mapsto(\Id_{d}-CC^{\top}S)\eta.\end{split}

(3.23)

Observe that this is obtained by ignoring the antisymmetric part of the projection to the Stiefel because this part maps vectors to
V_{C}
and
V_{C}\cap H_{C}=\quantity{0_{d \x N}}
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\gradd{f}([C])=(\Id_{d}-CC^{\top}S)S^{-1}\gradd{\overline{\overline{f}}}(C).

(3.24)

Example 2.108
.

Using Proposition
2.101
and the previous example, we obtain the following equation for the gradient of a function
f:\text{Gr}(N_{1},d_{1})\times\text{Gr}(N_{2},d_{2})\to\mathbf{R}
:

\gradd{f}([C_{1}],[C_{2}])=\quantity(\proj_1S_1^{-1}\grad{\oll{f}}(\cdot, C_2)(C_1), \ \proj_2S_2^{-1}\grad{\oll{f}}(C_1, \cdot)(C_2)),

(3.25)

being
\proj_{i}=\Id_{d_{i}}-C_{i}C_{i}^{\top}S_{i}
and
\gradd\overline{\overline{f}}
the Euclidean gradient of the extension of the lifting.

Definition 2.109
.

Given a vector field
V
, we define a function called
derivation
in the following way:

\displaystyle\begin{split}DV:C^{\infty}(X)&\to C^{\infty}(X)\\
f&\mapsto\quantity(x \mapsto T_xf(V(x))).\end{split}

(3.26)

DV
is also a linear transformation over
\mathbf{R}
and it satisfies the following Leibniz rule:

DV(fg)=fDV(g)+gDV(f).

(3.27)

Definition 2.110
.

Given a manifold
X
, an
(affine) connection on
X
is a function

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla:\mathfrak{X}(X)\times\mathfrak{X}(X)&\to\mathfrak{X}(X)\\
(V_{1},V_{2})&\mapsto\nabla_{V_{1}}V_{2}\end{split}

(3.28)

that satisfies the following axioms for every
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
and
f,g\in C^{\infty}(X)
:

1.

\nabla_{fV_{1}+gV_{2}}V_{3}=f\nabla_{V_{1}}V_{3}+g\nabla_{V_{2}}V_{3}
.

2.

\nabla_{V_{1}}(fV_{2})=f\nabla_{V_{1}}V_{2}+DV_{1}(f)V_{2}
.

The vector field
\nabla_{V_{1}}V_{2}
is called the
covariant derivative of
V_{2}
in the direction
V_{1}
.

Observation 2.111
.

The axioms above are saying that the connection is
C^{\infty}(X)
-linear in the first entry and that it satisfies a generalized Leibniz rule in the second.

Proposition 2.112
.

Given three vector fields
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
such that
V_{1}(x)=V_{2}(x)
, then
(\nabla_{V_{1}}V_{3})(x)=(\nabla_{V_{2}}V_{3})(x)
.
In other words, the vector field
\nabla_{V_{1}}V_{3}
computed at
x
just depends on the vector
V_{1}(x)
and not on the whole vector field
V_{1}
.

Example 2.113
.

When
X
is a vector space, the affine connection is given by the directional derivative of vector fields.
So, if
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
, we define:

\nabla_{V_{1}}V_{2}(x)\coloneqq\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{V_{2}(x+tV_{1}(x))-V_{2}(x)}{t}.

(3.29)

Again, this is only possible because we identify
T_{x}X\cong X
, otherwise
x+tV_{1}(x)
does not make any sense.

Example 2.114
.

Let
X
,
Y
be manifolds and
\nabla^{X}
,
\nabla^{Y}
be connections on
X
and
Y
, respectively.
Observe that there exists a diffeomorphism between the tangent bundles
T(X\times Y)
and
TX\times TY
given by
((x,y),[\gamma])\mapsto((x,[\pi_{X}\circ\gamma]),(y,[\pi_{Y}\circ\gamma]))
.
Consequently, we can assume vector fields
V\in\mathfrak{X}(X\times Y)
are given by
(V_{X},V_{Y})
, being
V_{X}:X\times Y\to TX
and
V_{Y}:X\times Y\to TY
.
Having said that, if we fix
(x,y)\in X\times Y
, we define a connection on
X\times Y
as follows:

\quantity(\nabla_{(V_X, V_Y)}^{X \x Y}(W_X, W_Y))(x,y)\coloneqq\bigg(\nabla_{V_{X}(x,y)}^{X}W_{X}(\cdot,y)+T_{y}W_{X}(x,\cdot)(V_{Y}(x,y)),\\
T_{x}W_{Y}(\cdot,y)(V_{X}(x,y))+\nabla_{V_{Y}(x,y)}^{Y}W_{Y}(x,\cdot)\bigg).

(3.30)

This is a little cumbersome, so, let us break into pieces.
First,
W(\cdot,y):X\to TX
is a vector field on
X
and we are abusing notation and considering
V_{X}(x,y)
as a tangent vector in
T_{x}X
.
So,
\nabla_{V_{X}(x,y)}^{X}W_{X}(\cdot,y)
is the usual covariant derivative on
X
.
Now,
T_{y}W_{X}(x,\cdot)(V_{Y}(x,y))
is trickier.
Observe that
W_{X}(x,\cdot):Y\to T_{x}X
can be seen a smooth function because
T_{x}X
is a vector space and, consequently, a manifold.
But that means we can compute the directional derivative of
W_{X}(x,\cdot)
, which is
T_{y}W_{X}(x,\cdot):T_{y}Y\to T_{W_{X}(x,y)}T_{x}X
.
Using the isomorphism
T_{W_{X}(x,y)}T_{x}X\cong T_{x}X
, we obtain the desired, since now
T_{y}W_{X}(x,\cdot)(V_{Y}(x,y))
is indeed a vector on
T_{x}X
.

Definition 2.115
.

Given two vector fields
V_{1},V_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
, we define the
Lie bracket of
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}[V_{1},V_{2}]:C^{\infty}(X)&\to C^{\infty}(X)\\
f&\mapsto DV_{1}(DV_{2}(f))-DV_{2}(DV_{1}(f)),\end{split}

(3.31)

being
DV_{i}
the derivation induced by
V_{i}
.

Definition 2.116
.

Given a Riemannian manifold
(X,\innerproduct{\cdot}{\cdot})
, there exists a unique connection
\nabla
that satisfies the following two additional axioms for every
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
:

1.

[V_{1},V_{2}]=D\nabla_{V_{1}}V_{2}-D\nabla_{V_{2}}V_{1}
.

2.

DV_{1}\quantity(\braket{V_2}{V_3})=\innerproduct{\nabla_{V_1}V_2}{V_3}+\innerproduct{V_2}{\nabla_{V_1}V_3}
.

This unique connection is called
Levi-Civita connection
.

Observation 2.117
.

In the first axiom the notation
D\nabla_{V_{i}}V_{j}
represents the derivation induced by the field
\nabla_{V_{i}}V_{j}
.
In the second axiom
DV_{1}\quantity(\braket{V_2}{V_3})
should be interpreted as follows:
DV_{1}
is a derivation and
\innerproduct{V_2}{V_3}\in C^{\infty}(X)
is the function
x\mapsto\innerproduct{V_2(x)}{V_3(x)}_{x}
.
The right-hand side is also interpreted as a smooth function:
x\mapsto\innerproduct{\nabla_{V_1(x)}V_2}{V_3(x)}+\innerproduct{V_2(x)}{\nabla_{V_1(x)}V_3}
.

Example 2.118
.

The connection described for vector spaces is the Levi-Civita connection.

Proposition 2.119
.

If
X
is a Riemannian submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, then its Levi-Civita connection is given by:

\quantity(\nabla_{V_1}^XV_2)(x)=\proj_{x}^{X}\nabla_{V_{1}(x)}^{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\overline{V}_{2},

(3.32)

being
\overline{V}_{2}:\mathbf{R}^{d}\to\mathbf{R}^{d}
an extension of
V_{2}
.

Proposition 2.120
.

If
X/\sim
is a Riemannian quotient manifold of
X
and
X
is a Riemannian submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, then the Levi-Civita connection on
X/\sim
is given by:

\quantity(\nabla_{V_1}^{X/\sim}V_2)([x])=\proj_{x}^{\hor}\nabla_{\lift_{x}(V_{1}(x))}^{X}\overline{V}_{2},

(3.33)

being
\overline{V}_{2}
a horizontal lift of
V_{2}
.

Definition 2.121
.

Let
X
be a Riemannian manifold,
\nabla
be its Levi-Civita connection and
f:X\to\mathbf{R}
be a smooth function.
The
(Riemannian) Hessian of
f
is the (linear) operator

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hess{f}(x):T_{x}M&\to T_{x}M\\
v&\mapsto\nabla_{v}\gradd{f}.\end{split}

(3.34)

This operator is well-defined because of Proposition
2.112
.

Example 2.122
.

When
X
is a vector space, the Hessian is just

\hess{f}(x)(v)=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\gradd{f}(x+tv)-\gradd{f}(x)}{t}.

(3.35)

However, if we choose an orthonormal basis
B=\quantity{v_1, \ldots, v_d}
for
X
, we can represent the Hessian in the usual way as a matrix:

\hess{f}(x)(v)=\begin{bmatrix}\partialderivative{f}{v_1}{v_1}\quantity(x)&\ldots&\partialderivative{f}{v_1}{v_d}\quantity(x)\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\partialderivative{f}{v_d}{v_1}\quantity(x)&\ldots&\partialderivative{f}{v_d}{v_d}\quantity(x)\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\\
\vdots\\
c_{d}\end{bmatrix},

(3.36)

being
v=c_{1}v_{1}+\ldots+c_{d}v_{d}
.

Proposition 2.123
.

Let
f:X\times Y\to\mathbf{R}
be a smooth function defined on the product of two Riemannian manifolds.
Fixing
(x,y)\in X\times Y
, let
f_{1}
be the function
f(\cdot,y)
,
f_{2}
be the function
f(x,\cdot)
, and write
\gradd{f}(x,y)=(G_{X}(x,y),G_{Y}(x,y))
.
Then, the Riemannian Hessian of
f
is given by:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hess{f}(x,y)&:T_{x}X\times T_{y}Y\to T_{x}X\times T_{y}Y\\
(v,w)\mapsto\bigg(&\hess{f_{1}}(x)(v)+T_{y}(G_{X}(x,\cdot))(w),\ \\
&T_{x}(G_{Y}(\cdot,y))(v)+\hess{f_{2}}(y)(w)\bigg).\end{split}

(3.37)

Example 2.124
.

Let us consider the case in which
X=\text{Gr}(N_{1},d_{1})
and
Y=\text{Gr}(N_{2},d_{2})
.
By definition,

\hess{f_{1}}([C_{1}])(\eta_{1})=\nabla_{\eta_{1}}^{\text{Gr}(N_{1},d_{1})}\gradd{f_{1}}.

(3.38)

Now, according to Proposition
2.120
,

\nabla_{\eta_{1}}^{\text{Gr}(N_{1},d_{1})}\gradd{f_{1}}=\proj_{C_{1}}^{\hor}\nabla_{\lift_{C_{1}}(\eta_{1})}^{\text{St}(N_{1},d_{1})}\gradd{\overline{f}_{1}}.

(3.39)

However,

\gradd{\overline{f}_{1}}(\cdot)=(\proj_{(\cdot)}^{\hor}\circ S_{1}^{-1}\gradd{\overline{\overline{f}}_{1}})(\cdot).

(3.40)

So, in the end we have:

\hess{f_{1}}([C_{1}])(\eta_{1})=\proj_{C_{1}}^{\hor}\nabla_{\lift_{C_{1}}(\eta_{1})}^{\text{Mat}(d_{1}\times N_{1})}\quantity(\proj_{(\cdot)}^{\hor} \circ S_1^{-1}\grad{\oll{f}_1}(\cdot)).

(3.41)

Now we can compute this covariant derivative using Calculus because it is defined in the Euclidean space.
Since
\proj_{C_{1}}^{\hor}=\Id_{d_{1}}-C_{1}C_{1}^{\top}S_{1}
and
S_{1}^{-1}\gradd{\overline{\overline{f}}_{1}}
are matrices, we can basically use the product rule for derivative and the final result is:

\proj_{C_{1}}^{\hor}\nabla_{\lift_{C_{1}}(\eta_{1})}^{\text{Mat}(d_{1}\times N_{1})}\quantity(\proj_{(\cdot)}^{\hor} \circ S_1^{-1}\grad{\oll{f}_1}(\cdot))=\proj_{C_{1}}^{\hor}\hess{\overline{\overline{f}}_{1}}(C_{1})\eta_{1}-\eta_{1}C_{1}^{\top}\gradd{\overline{\overline{f}}_{1}}(C_{1}).

(3.42)

Now let us compute
T_{C_{2}}\quantity(G_{\gr{N_1}{d_1}}(C_1, \cdot))(\eta_{2})
.
Using Proposition
2.101
and Example
2.107
, we have

G_{\text{Gr}(N_{1},d_{1})}(C_{1},\cdot)=\proj_{C_{1}}^{\hor}S_{1}^{-1}\gradd{\overline{\overline{f}}}(C_{1},\cdot).

(3.43)

However, since we are computing the directional derivative with respect to
C_{2}
, in this case we do not need to differentiate the projection.
Consequently,

T_{C_{2}}\quantity(G_{\gr{N_1}{d_1}}(C_1, \cdot))(\eta_{2})=\proj_{C_{1}}^{\hor}S_{1}^{-1}\hess{\overline{\overline{f}}}(C_{1},\cdot)(C_{2})\eta_{2}.

(3.44)

So, if we define
\hess_{11}{\overline{\overline{f}}}(C_{1},C_{2})\coloneqq\hess{\overline{\overline{f}}_{1}}(C_{1})
,
\hess_{12}{\overline{\overline{f}}}(C_{1},C_{2})\coloneqq\hess{\overline{\overline{f}}}(C_{1},\cdot)(C_{2})
and
\proj_{i}\coloneqq\proj_{C_{i}}^{\hor}
, the final result is:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hess{f}([C_{1}],[C_{2}])&:T_{[C_{1}]}\text{Gr}(N_{1},d_{1})\times T_{[C_{2}]}\text{Gr}(N_{2},d_{2})\to T_{[C_{1}]}\text{Gr}(N_{1},d_{1})\times T_{[C_{2}]}\text{Gr}(N_{2},d_{2})\\
(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\mapsto\Big(&\proj_{1}S_{1}^{-1}\big(\hess_{11}\overline{\overline{f}}(C_{1},C_{2})\eta_{1}+\hess_{12}\overline{\overline{f}}(C_{1},C_{2})\eta_{2}\big)-\eta_{1}C_{1}^{\top}\gradd\overline{\overline{f}}_{1}(C_{1},C_{2}),\\
&\proj_{2}S_{2}^{-1}\big(\hess_{21}\overline{\overline{f}}(C_{1},C_{2})\eta_{1}+\hess_{22}\overline{\overline{f}}(C_{1},C_{2})\eta_{2}\big)-\eta_{2}C_{2}^{\top}\gradd\overline{\overline{f}}_{2}(C_{1},C_{2})\Big),\end{split}

(3.45)

Definition 2.125
.

Given a manifold
X
and a (smooth) curve
\gamma:(a,b)\to X
, a
(smooth) vector field along
\gamma
is a smooth function
V:(a,b)\to TX
such that
V(t)\in T_{\gamma(t)}X
for every
t\in(a,b)
.
The set of all vector fields along
\gamma
will be denoted by
\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
.

Observation 2.126
.

Again,
\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
is actually a
C^{\infty}((a,b))
-module.

Definition 2.127
.

Given a Riemannian manifold
X
, a curve
\gamma:(a,b)\to X
and the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
, there exists a unique (linear) operator
\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}:\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)\to\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
such that the following axioms hold for every
V_{1}\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
,
V_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
and
f\in C^{\infty}((a,b))
:

1.

\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}(fV_{1})=f^{\prime}V_{1}+f\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}(V_{1})
.

2.

\quantity(\frac{\nabla}{\dd{t}}(V_2 \circ\gamma))(t)=\nabla_{\gamma^{\prime}(t)}V_{2}
.

The operator
\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}
is called
covariant derivative along
\gamma
.

Example 2.128
.

When
X
is a vector space, we can compute the covariant derivative along a curve as a regular derivative, i.e., given
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
, we have

\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}(V)(t)=V^{\prime}(t)\coloneqq\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{V(t+h)-V(t)}{h}.

(3.46)

Proposition 2.129
.

If
X
is a Riemannian submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the covariant derivative of
V
along
\gamma
is given by:

\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}(V)(t)=\proj_{\gamma(t)}^{X}V^{\prime}(t),

(3.47)

being
\proj_{\gamma(t)}^{X}
the projection to
T_{\gamma(t)}X
.

Proposition 2.130
.

If
X/\sim
is a Riemannian quotient manifold of
X
and
X
is a Riemannian submanifold of
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the covariant derivative of
V
along
\gamma
is given by:

\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}(V)(t)=\proj_{\gamma(t)}^{\hor}\overline{V}^{\prime}(t),

(3.48)

being
\proj_{\gamma(t)}^{\hor}
the projection to
H_{\gamma(t)}
and
\overline{V}
the horizontal lift of
V
along
\gamma
, i.e.,
\overline{V}(t)\coloneqq\lift_{\gamma(t)}(V(t))
.

Definition 2.131
.

Given a curve
\gamma:(a,b)\to X
, we define the
acceleration of
\gamma
to be
\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

Definition 2.132
.

A curve
\gamma:(a,b)\to X
is said to be a
geodesic
if
\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}(\gamma^{\prime})(t)=0
for every
t\in(a,b)
.

Definition 2.133
.

A vector field
V
along
\gamma:(a,b)\to X
is said to be
parallel
if
\frac{\nabla}{dt}(V)(t)=0
for every
t\in(a,b)
.

Theorem 2.134
.

Given a manifold
X
, a connection
\nabla
on
X
, a curve
\gamma:(a,b)\to X
,
t_{0}\in(a,b)
and
v\in T_{\gamma(t_{0})}X
, there exists a unique parallel vector field
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
such that
V(t_{0})=v
.

Definition 2.135
.

Given a curve
\gamma:(a,b)\to X
and
t_{0},t_{1}\in(a,b)
, we define the
parallel transport along
\gamma
to be the linear transformation defined by:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\pt_{t_{0}\to t_{1}}^{\gamma}:T_{\gamma(t_{0})}X&\to T_{\gamma(t_{1})}X\\
v&\mapsto V(t_{1}),\end{split}

(3.49)

being
V\in\mathfrak{X}(\gamma)
the unique parallel vector field along
\gamma
such that
V(t_{0})=v
(which exists by the previous theorem).

Observation 2.136
.

From now on we will assume
[0,1]\subset(a,b)
,
t_{0}=0
and
t_{1}=1
.

Example 2.137
.

Geodesics on vector spaces are straight lines.
Indeed, according to Example
2.128
,
\frac{\nabla}{\differential{t}}\gamma^{\prime}=\gamma^{\prime\prime}
.
Consequently, if we assume
\gamma(0)=x
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=v
, we have that
\gamma(t)=x+tv
by the Picard–Lindelöf Theorem (also known as Existence and Uniqueness of Solutions to Ordinary Differential Equations).
Now, the parallel transport is just the identity function because the vector field obtained in the previous theorem is unique and the constant vector field obviously satisfies
V(0)=v
.

Example 2.138
.

Now let us compute geodesics and the parallel transport for the Grassmannian.
According to Proposition
2.130
, the covariant derivative of a vector field
W
along a curve
\gamma
in the Grassmannian
\text{Gr}(N,d)
is given by:

\quantity(\frac{\nabla}{\dd{t}}W)(t)=\proj_{\gamma(t)}^{\hor}\overline{W}^{\prime}(t).

(3.50)

Therefore, this is
0
(that is,
W
is parallel) if, and only if,
\overline{W}^{\prime}(t)\in V_{\gamma(t)}
(recall that
V_{\gamma(t)}=H_{\gamma(t)}^{\perp}
), which means

\overline{W}^{\prime}(t)=\gamma(t)\nu(t)

(3.51)

for
\nu(t)
antisymmetric.
However,
\overline{W}(t)\in H_{\gamma(t)}
for every
t
, which means

\gamma(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t)=0.

(3.52)

Differentiating this, we obtain

\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t)+\gamma(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}^{\prime}(t)=0

(3.53)

and, consequently,

\nu(t)=-\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t)

(3.54)

because
\gamma(t)^{\top}S\gamma(t)=\Id_{N}
.
In other words,
\overline{W}
is parallel to
\gamma
if, and only if,

\overline{W}^{\prime}(t)=-\gamma(t)\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t).

(3.55)

Now, if
\overline{W}=\gamma^{\prime}
, this amounts to

\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)=-\gamma(t)\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\gamma^{\prime}(t).

(3.56)

Therefore, assuming that
\gamma
is a geodesic, the above holds and
\gamma(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t)=\gamma(t)^{\top}S\gamma^{\prime}(t)=0
because
\overline{W}(t)\in H_{\gamma(t)}
.
But that means
\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t)
is constant because

\derivative{t}\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t)=\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t)+\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}^{\prime}(t)=-\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\gamma^{\prime}(t)\underbrace{\gamma(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t)}_{0}\\
-\underbrace{\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\gamma(t)}_{0}\gamma^{\prime}(t)^{\top}S\overline{W}(t).

(3.57)

Consequently, the ODE we need to solve for the geodesic is

\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)=-\gamma(t)\gamma^{\prime}(0)^{\top}S\gamma^{\prime}(0)=-\gamma(t)\kappa,

(3.58)

which is an harmonic oscillator.
Now, suppose we have an invertible matrix
O
such that
O^{\top}SO=\Id_{d}
.
Then
S=O^{-\top}O^{-1}
.
So, let
O^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(0)=UDV
be a
thin Singular Value Decomposition
6
6

6

Assuming
N\leq d
, which is always the case for us,
U\in\text{Mat}(d\times N)
and
D,V\in\text{Mat}(N\times N)
are matrices such that
D
is diagonal and
U^{\top}U=V^{\top}V=VV^{\top}=\Id_{N}
. The matrix
U
is also known as semi-orthogonal because it is not true that
UU^{\top}=\Id_{d}
if
N<d
.
(see
[
24
, Chapter 2]
) and observe that

\gamma(t)=\quantity(\gamma(0)V^{\top}\cos(tD) + OU\sin(tD))V

(3.59)

satisfies the equation above.
Indeed, using that
V^{\top}=V^{-1}
and that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma^{\prime}(0)^{\top}S\gamma^{\prime}(0)&=\gamma^{\prime}(0)^{\top}O^{-\top}O^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(0)\\
&=(O^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(0))^{\top}O^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(0)\\
&=(UDV)^{\top}UDV\\
&=V^{\top}DU^{\top}UDV\\
&=V^{\top}D^{2}V,\end{split}

(3.60)

we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)&=\quantity(-\gamma(0)V^{\top}\cos(tD)D^2 - OU\sin(tD)D^2)V\\
&=-\quantity(\gamma(0)V^{\top}\cos(tD) + OU\sin(tD))D^{2}V\\
&=-\quantity(\gamma(0)V^{\top}\cos(tD) + OU\sin(tD))VV^{-1}D^{2}V\\
&=-\quantity(\gamma(0)V^{\top}\cos(tD) + OU\sin(tD))VV^{\top}D^{2}V\\
&=-\gamma(t)\gamma^{\prime}(0)^{\top}S\gamma^{\prime}(0).\end{split}

(3.61)

So, that is it, we obtained a closed formula for the geodesic.

Now, the ODE we need to solve for the parallel transport along a geodesic is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{W}^{\prime}(t)&=-\gamma(t)\gamma^{\prime}(0)^{\top}S\overline{W}(0)\\
&=-\quantity(\gamma(0)V^{\top}\cos(tD) + OU\sin(tD))V\gamma^{\prime}(0)^{\top}S\overline{W}(0).\end{split}

(3.62)

Integrating this, we obtain:

\overline{W}(t)=\quantity(-\gamma(0)V^{\top}\sin(tD)D^{-1}
+ OU\cos(tD)D^{-1})V\gamma^{\prime}(0)^{\top}S\overline{W}(0)+\kappa\\
=\quantity(-\gamma(0)V^{\top}\sin(tD)D^{-1}
+ OU\cos(tD)D^{-1})V(O^{-1}\gamma^{\prime}(0))^{\top}O^{-1}\overline{W}(0)+\kappa\\
=\quantity(-\gamma(0)V^{\top}\sin(tD)D^{-1}
+ OU\cos(tD)D^{-1})DU^{\top}O^{-1}\overline{W}(0)+\kappa\\
=\quantity(-\gamma(0)V^{\top}\sin(tD)
+ OU\cos(tD))U^{\top}O^{-1}\overline{W}(0)+\kappa.

(3.63)

But
\overline{W}(0)=\overline{W}(0)
, therefore,

\overline{W}(0)=\quantity(-\gamma(0)V^{\top}\sin(0 \cdot D)
+ OU\cos(0 \cdot D))U^{\top}O^{-1}\overline{W}(0)+\kappa\\
=OUU^{\top}O^{-1}\overline{W}(0)+\kappa.

(3.64)

Since the solution is unique, if we choose
\kappa=\quantity(\Id- OUU^{\top}O^{-1})\overline{W}(0)
, we obtain the result that the parallel transport of a vector
\eta
along a geodesic is given by:

P_{0\to 1}^{\gamma}(\eta)=\quantity(\qty(-\gamma(0)V^{\top}\sin(tD)
+ OU\cos(tD))U^{\top}O^{-1}
+ \qty(\Id- OUU^{\top}O^{-1}))\eta

(3.65)

When
\eta=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
, the transport becomes

P_{0\to 1}^{\gamma}(\gamma^{\prime}(0))=\quantity(-\gamma(0)V^{\top}\sin(tD)
+ OU\cos(tD))DV.

(3.66)

Definition 2.139
.

A
retraction on
X
is a smooth function
R:TX\to X
such that the curve
\rho(t)\coloneqq R(x,t[\gamma])
satisfies
[\gamma]=[\rho]
for every
(x,[\gamma])\in TX
.

Theorem 2.140
.

Given a Riemannian manifold
X
and
(x,v)\in TX
, there exists a unique
maximal
geodesic
\gamma_{v}:(a,b)\to X
such that (1)
0\in(a,b)
, (2)
\gamma_{v}(0)=x
and (3)
\gamma_{v}^{\prime}(0)=v
.
By maximal it is meant that, if
\rho_{v}:(c,d)\to X
also satisfies these three properties, then
(c,d)\subset(a,b)
.

Definition 2.141
.

Let

\mathcal{O}\coloneqq\quantity{(x, v) \in TX : \text{$\gamma_v$ is defined on an interval containing $[0,1]$}}.

(3.67)

The
(Riemannian) exponential
is the function
\exp:\mathcal{O}\to X
defined by

\exp(x,tv)=\gamma_{v}(t).

(3.68)

When
x
is fixed, we will denote the function
\exp(x, \cdot)
by
\exp_{x}
.

Definition 2.142
.

A Riemannian manifold
X
is said to
complete
if for every
(x,v)\in TX
we have
\gamma_{v}
defined on the whole
\mathbf{R}
.

Observation 2.143
.

If the manifold
X
is complete, then
\mathcal{O}=TX
.

Proposition 2.144
.

The Riemannian exponential is a retraction.

Example 2.145
.

If
X
is a vector space, the Riemannian exponential is given by

\exp(x,tv)=\exp_{x}(v)=x+tv.

(3.69)

Example 2.146
.

In the case of the Grassmannian
\text{Gr}(N,d)
, the exponential is given by

\exp([C], t\eta)=\exp_{[C]}(t\eta)=\quantity(CV^{\top}\cos(tD) + OU\sin(tD))V,

(3.70)

being
O^{-1}\eta=UDV
a thin Singular Value Decomposition.
See Example
2.138
for the details.

Proposition 2.147
.

The Riemannian exponential in the product of manifolds
X\times Y
is given by

\exp^{X\times Y}((x,y),t(v,w))=(\exp^{X}(x,tv),\exp^{Y}(y,tw)).

(3.71)

To conclude, let us state without proof that the Grassmannian is a complete manifold.
This fact is important because one of the hypothesis of the theorems that assert the algorithms we will implement converge is that the manifold should be complete.

Theorem 2.148

(Hopf–Rinow)
.

A compact and connected Riemannian manifold is complete.

Observation 2.149
.

This is actually a corollary of Hopf–Rinow that is more suited to our purposes.
If the reader is interested in the “original” theorem, see
[
32
, Theorem 6.19]
.

Observation 2.150
.

Connectedness is a property of topological spaces that says we cannot break the space into two or more disjoint pieces (if the space is connected, of course).
A geometric example of a space that is
not
connected is two disjoint open balls in the Euclidean plane, such as
B((-2,0),1)\cup B((2,0),1)
.
The Grassmannian is connected, but we do not know any elementary proof of this fact and we refer the interested reader to
[
40
, Section 2.4]
for a proof.

Corollary 2.151
.

The Grassmannian and the product of Grassmannians is complete.

Proof.

It follows from Hopf–Rinow together with the fact that the Grassmannian and its finite products are connected and compact spaces (Corollary
6.67
).
∎

3
Quantum Mechanics
